56	
57	\begin{abstract}
58	Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with DLinear on $107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
59	\end{abstract}
60	
61	\section{Introduction}
62	\label{sec:intro}
63	
64	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions; this paper studies the adapter, holding the backbone fixed.
65	
66	Standard TSFM adapters~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora} pool hidden states and project them to the forecast horizon through the same head for every window (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}a). Real time series mix heterogeneous regimes (quiet baselines, seasonal excursions, abrupt shape changes), and instance normalization handles only the scale-and-offset component of that heterogeneity; residual \emph{shape} heterogeneity (periodicity, trends, level shifts, change points) is encoded in the hidden states across layers, as recent probing analyses of MOMENT and related TSFMs have shown~\citep{wilinski2025tsfm_representations, pandey2025tsfm_semantics}, motivating a per-window mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical: a shared TSFM in GPU memory can serve many tenants through lightweight per-task adapters hot-swapped from host RAM, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
67	
68	This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes (formalized in Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}; signal-ratio diagnostic in Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
69	
70	The failure is specific to instance normalization. Backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), and standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recover at most $10.9\%$ MSE, still $2.7\times$ worse than RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
71	
72	Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix principle is direct. \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen; only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so within a fixed MoE architecture frozen should match or beat unfrozen, which we confirm empirically (frozen best on $4/6$, unfrozen-wins bounded at $\leq 13\%$).
73	
74	\paragraph{Contributions.}
75	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, itemsep=0.15em, topsep=0.2em]
76	\item We identify \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: an architectural failure mode of MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs that is upstream of the optimization-induced collapses targeted by Switch, ST-MoE, ReMoE, and Expert-Choice. Eight causal controls (RevIN ablation, BatchNorm/GroupNorm swaps, router-input interpolation, vision cross-modality, and others) converge on the normalizer as cause.
77	\item We formalize the mechanism via a mutual-information decomposition (Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) and derive a tractable signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ that anticipates which datasets benefit from raw routing at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$), including boundary cases where it correctly does not (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The predictor generalizes across three normalization families, five backbones, and a vision modality.
78	\item We propose RR-MoA, the \textbf{minimal causal intervention}: changing only the router's input from normalized hidden states to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$, with no change to the optimizer, the experts, or the frozen backbone. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against AdaMix (the same architecture with collapsed routing), strictly dominates $11$ optimization-side rescue mechanisms ($720$ runs; best rescue recovers $\leq 10.9\%$, RR-MoA $2.7{\times}$ better), and is not subsumed by naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection ($+42$--$239\%$ MSE; $11/18$ cells re-collapse). Two architecturally distinct variants confirm the principle generalizes beyond RR-MoA's specific router: \textbf{SR-MoA} (self-routed, no external router; outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets) and \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (expert-level raw-input dual-stream; matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets, generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones).
79	\end{itemize}

# Audit Report: Unsupported Claims in main.tex

I read the full paper (Abstract, §1 Introduction, §2 Related Work, §3 Method, §4 Experiments, §5 Conclusion, plus appendices §A–§P) and the inline `\bibitem` list. The paper is well-supported overall — most numeric claims trace to a Table/Figure/Appendix reference, and most empirical claims about prior work cite a `\bibitem`. The findings below flag the residual concerns.

---

## §1 Introduction (lines 61–80)

### MEDIUM — Line 64
**Snippet:** "Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, Chronos, Timer-XL, and Moirai now provide pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}."
**Concern:** "billion-parameter scales" is a claim about scope that includes UniTS (`lee2024units`) and Lag-Llama (`rasul2024lagllama`), which are **not** billion-parameter models — only Time-MoE and Timer-S1 are. A reviewer who checks the cited papers will see UniTS/Lag-Llama in the wrong bucket.
**Remedy:** Soften to "with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{shi2024timemoe, liu2026timers1}" and cite UniTS/Lag-Llama/TTM separately as additional pretrained TSFMs.

### LOW — Line 66
**Snippet:** "Standard TSFM adapters~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora} pool hidden states and project them to the forecast horizon through the same head for every window"
**Concern:** Citing `hu2022lora` (LoRA) as a "standard TSFM adapter" is a stretch — LoRA is general PEFT, not TSFM-specific. The other three citations are fine.
**Remedy:** Drop `hu2022lora` here; it's already cited later for the LoRA baseline.

### LOW — Line 66
**Snippet:** "...as recent probing analyses of MOMENT and related TSFMs have shown~\citep{wilinski2025tsfm_representations, pandey2025tsfm_semantics}, motivating a per-window mixture of expert adapters"
**Concern:** Phrasing implies the cited probing work motivates the mixture-of-experts approach; the cited works actually show what TSFMs encode in hidden states, not that MoE is the right response. Soft over-claim.
**Remedy:** Reword as "...as recent probing analyses of MOMENT and related TSFMs have shown. This residual heterogeneity motivates..."

### MEDIUM — Line 68
**Snippet:** "When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations"
**Concern:** "the standard MoE recipe" is presented as a specific, well-defined method via two citations, but Switch (fedus2022switch) routes between FFN experts in language models, while AdaMix is for adapters. This is a mild oversimplification, though the table reference (`tab:adamix`) is provided immediately after, so the empirical claim is anchored.
**Remedy:** Acceptable as is; could read "the standard MoE adapter recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix} with load-balancing~\citep{fedus2022switch}".

### LOW — Line 76
**Snippet:** "an architectural failure mode of MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs that is upstream of the optimization-induced collapses targeted by Switch, ST-MoE, ReMoE, and Expert-Choice"
**Concern:** The names appear without `\cite{}` keys at this contribution-list location, although they are cited elsewhere in §2. NeurIPS reviewers expect citations on first textual mention.
**Remedy:** Add inline `\citep{fedus2022switch, zoph2022stmoe, wang2025remoe, zhou2022expertchoice}`.

## §2 Related Work (lines 228–236)

### MEDIUM — Line 230
**Snippet:** "the closest precedent in spirit is heterogeneous mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting, whereas, to our knowledge, no prior TSFM work uses a per-window mixture over distinct adapter topologies"
**Concern:** "the closest precedent in spirit is heterogeneous mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting" — this asserts a specific prior work exists but offers no citation. The "to our knowledge, no prior TSFM work uses..." is also a strong negative claim that reviewers will probe.
**Remedy:** Cite the LLM-side heterogeneous-MoE precedent (e.g., AdaMix, MoLE, MoLA, or HydraLoRA depending on which is meant); soften "no prior TSFM work" to "no prior TSFM work we surveyed".

### MEDIUM — Line 232
**Snippet:** "stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy on the datasets we tested (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe})"
**Concern:** The reference is correct; this is fine. (Listed for completeness — no issue.)

### LOW — Line 234
**Snippet:** "Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports the broader view that the router's input matters: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses."
**Concern:** "supports the broader view that the router's input matters" — these papers are about routerless MoEs, not specifically about input choice. Mild over-attribution to these citations.
**Remedy:** Reword as "concurrent work toward router-free MoEs ... reaches related conclusions about restructuring the routing decision".

### LOW — Line 234
**Snippet:** "\citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs."
**Concern:** "proves" is strong for an arXiv paper (`wang2026myth`, arXiv:2604.09780). The cited work likely *argues* or *shows*, not *proves* in the formal-theorem sense.
**Remedy:** Replace "proves" with "shows" or "argues".

## §4 Experiments (lines 386–656)

### HIGH — Line 392
**Snippet:** "Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} as no-instance-normalization negative controls"
**Concern:** Calling Chronos and Timer-XL "no-instance-normalization" is asserted as fact about other systems' architectures. Reviewers will check. Chronos uses T5 architecture (LayerNorm-only is correct); Timer-XL uses LayerNorm only per the project notes. This is empirically correct but is presented as a load-bearing claim with no citation pointing to the architectural detail (the bibliographic citation is for the model itself, not for the normalization claim).
**Remedy:** Either cite a section in those papers (or your own Appendix~\ref{app:setup} which lists architectural specs), or hedge: "Chronos and Timer-XL serve as negative controls (LayerNorm only, no per-window instance normalization; see Appendix~\ref{app:setup})".

### MEDIUM — Line 394
**Snippet:** "AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} (hidden-state MoE)"
**Concern:** AdaMix in `wang2022adamix` is a stochastic-gating mixture-of-adapters baseline; the paper here uses a learned hidden-state softmax router which is one of several configurations (the AdaMix paper uses random selection for adaptation, not always learned softmax). The paper does disclose the specific implementation in Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}, but a reviewer reading just the main body might dispute that "AdaMix" = "learned hidden-state MoE".
**Remedy:** Already partly addressed via "(hidden-state MoE)" parenthetical and the appendix; consider "AdaMix-style hidden-state-routed MoE~\citep{wang2022adamix}" to be more precise.

### MEDIUM — Line 462
**Snippet:** "AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} adapted to a learned hidden-state softmax router, the standard recipe across modern MoE adapters"
**Concern:** "the standard recipe across modern MoE adapters" is a categorical claim about the field with no citation set backing it. Reviewers will ask which papers count as "modern MoE adapters" and whether they all use this recipe.
**Remedy:** Soften to "a common recipe in modern MoE adapters" or cite a small set ("e.g., \citep{wang2022adamix, wu2024lora-mole, ...}") demonstrating prevalence.

### LOW — Line 462
**Snippet:** "BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}"
**Concern:** The appendix table only tests BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm at one freeze level (last-4) on ETTh1 only (Table `tab:norm_generalization`), with a single seed. The main-body claim "produce the same collapse" generalizes from that single experiment. Not enough seeds to firmly support "the same".
**Remedy:** Already pointed to the appendix; acceptable but consider hedging "produce the same collapse on ETTh1 (Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization})".

### MEDIUM — Line 481
**Snippet:** "on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, consistent with Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction"
**Concern:** Traffic isn't shown in the main 6-dataset grid; the reader may want to verify the Traffic result. Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio} shows Traffic at +2.9% as a boundary case, so this is anchored, but the main-text claim "RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters" should explicitly point at the figure.
**Remedy:** Add "(Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}, Traffic point)" in line.

### HIGH — Line 485 (Frozen Paradox decomposition)
**Snippet:** "frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$–$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox})"
**Concern:** Table `tab:baselines` only shows 3 datasets (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather); Figure `fig:frozen_paradox` (line 599) shows 6 datasets (the bar chart). Saying "Table~\ref{tab:baselines}" alongside the 6-dataset claim is misleading. The figure is the load-bearing reference.
**Remedy:** Drop `tab:baselines` from this citation, leave only `Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}`.

### MEDIUM — Line 485
**Snippet:** "A $90$-config hyperparameter tune (LR $10^{-5}$–$10^{-6}$, $100$ epochs, cosine + warmup, layerwise decay, batch up to $512$) still loses by $51$–$71\%$, ruling out optimization confounds."
**Concern:** "ruling out optimization confounds" is strong language. The 90-config tune covers a specific subspace; it doesn't formally rule out all optimization confounds. The number anchors to Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft} which is correct, but the categorical phrasing overreaches.
**Remedy:** Soften to "ruling out the optimization confounds covered by this sweep" or "consistent with optimization not being the bottleneck".

### LOW — Line 492
**Snippet:** "Independent ensembles are $37$–$46\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}): learned routing, not mere diversity, drives gains."
**Concern:** Table `tab:baselines` independent-ensemble numbers (1.081 / 1.049 / 0.509 vs RR-MoA 0.680 / 0.564 / 0.276) yield ~37–55% deltas. Worth verifying the 37–46% range matches a specific 3-dataset average vs the 6-dataset claim made earlier. Could be a small arithmetic mismatch.
**Remedy:** Cross-check; if it's the 6-dataset range from the broader sweep, point to Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa} explicitly.

### MEDIUM — Line 494
**Snippet:** "Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii)"
**Concern:** "confirms" is strong. The +67.7% increase is consistent with Observation 1 but is also consistent with several other explanations (e.g., the load-balance term overwhelming task loss). "Confirms" should be "is consistent with".
**Remedy:** "...consistent with Observation 1(ii)..."

### MEDIUM — Line 496
**Snippet:** "SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$–$42\%$ across all 6 datasets ($252$ runs, six backbones)"
**Concern:** Table `tab:self_routed` shows SR-MoA results, but a reader has to compute the 13–42% delta vs RR-MoA themselves. The numbers are not directly readable as a Δ% column. Anchored but not transparent.
**Remedy:** Either add a Δ% column to `tab:self_routed`, or point at a verify.py check that computes this. (The CLAUDE.md mentions `analyze_srmoa_strengthen.py` which does this aggregation.)

### MEDIUM — Line 496
**Snippet:** "on Timer-XL (no RevIN) the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$–$9\%$ ($15$–$100{\times}$ smaller)"
**Concern:** The table (lines 1499-1505) shows Timer-XL +4%, +8%, +9% on ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather. The "15-100× smaller" needs back-of-envelope: max MOMENT delta 768%, max Timer 9% → 85×; min MOMENT 87% / max Timer 9% → ~10×. Range "15-100×" is in the right ballpark but worth explicit derivation.
**Remedy:** Could add a verify.py check; otherwise acceptable.

### HIGH — Line 552
**Snippet:** "DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows): the gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline (k-NN/Ridge diagnostics, Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}), not adapter capacity."
**Concern:** "the gap reflects information destruction ... not adapter capacity" is a causal claim. Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic} (Table `tab:diagnostic`) shows backbone-feature k-NN regression is 16-33% worse than raw-input k-NN, which is consistent but does not formally rule out adapter capacity as a contributing factor. The dichotomy ("not adapter capacity") is over-strong.
**Remedy:** Soften: "the gap is consistent with information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline rather than insufficient adapter capacity".

### LOW — Line 554
**Snippet:** "A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a phase transition (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; $150$ runs)"
**Concern:** CLAUDE.md says the dose-response sweep is 180 runs (and figure caption later says "5 seeds × 6 datasets"). 5 × 6 × 5 alphas = 150 runs in main text, vs 180 elsewhere. Inconsistency.
**Remedy:** Verify whether 150 or 180 is correct and align all mentions (line 554, Appendix L, CLAUDE.md notes).

**Snippet:** "The threshold is quantitatively predicted at $\alpha^* = R(\mathcal{D})/(R(\mathcal{D})+1) \approx 0.40$ for the median benchmark (Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth})"
**Concern:** "quantitatively predicted" is strong; Proposition `prop:phase_transition` derives this as a *heuristic SNR onset*, and the paper itself (line 1144, line 1159) labels it heuristic. The main-body sentence overstates.
**Remedy:** "is heuristically predicted at..." or "the predicted soft-onset threshold is..."

**Snippet:** "$R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$"

**Concern:** Table `tab:cross_backbone` shows MOMENT-small at 3 datasets, MOMENT-large at 6, Moirai at 3 — that's 12 cells, not 21. The 21 must include Moirai-MoE or something else. Need to verify the count.

**Concern:** The "4/18 within chance" is not visible in any of the appendix tables I read. The Timer-XL table (`tab:timer_xl`) has 9 cells, with AdaMix winning 7/9. The 4/18 number is unlocatable in the audit.

## §5 Conclusion (lines 659–662)

### LOW — Line 662
**Snippet:** "When a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, the fix is restoring the input, not retuning the optimizer (replicated in a vision control, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe})"
**Concern:** Generalizing claim ("when a foundation model's...") on the strength of one vision result. Phrased as a meta-principle. The vision experiment (Table `tab:vision_moe`) does support the principle but is one cross-modality control. Reviewers may flag the universal phrasing.
**Remedy:** Hedge to "a pattern that replicates in a vision control" rather than asserting a universal rule.

### MEDIUM — Line 1129 (Proof Part iv)
**Snippet:** "high-$R$ datasets (Exchange, $R{=}2.17$) have $I(M,\Sigma; E) \gg \varepsilon$, producing large gains ($-68.5\%$); low-$R$ datasets (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$) have $I(M,\Sigma; E) \approx \varepsilon$, producing no gain ($+2.9\%$)."
**Concern:** "$I(M,\Sigma; E) \approx \varepsilon$" for Traffic is asserted as fact. Table `tab:mi_tightness` (line 1907) shows Traffic with H(E) = 0.63, H(E|S) = 0.44, retention 24.6% — this is computed from data, but the specific equation $I(M,\Sigma; E) \approx \varepsilon$ requires checking the gap value which the table reports as 0.02 (very small). So the claim is correct but the reasoning chain is implicit.
**Remedy:** Add a brief sentence pointing at the specific row of Table `tab:mi_tightness`.

**Snippet:** "The validated $\rho = -0.88$ correlation (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}) is empirical evidence \emph{for} this scaling rather than a direct corollary of the Gaussian-MI formula."
**Concern:** This is itself a hedge, fine. (Listed for completeness.)

### MEDIUM — Line 1141 (App D, IB connection)

**Concern:** "because spatial features are not the routing signal for classification" — this is a post-hoc explanation of why ResNet didn't collapse. While Table `tab:vision_moe` shows the empirical result, the *reason* (spatial features ≠ routing signal) is a hypothesis presented as fact.
**Remedy:** Reword as "consistent with spatial features not carrying the routing signal for classification".

This rules out H1."
**Concern:** "rules out H1" — the H1 hypothesis was about data scarcity; the experiment shows that more data + same recipe = worse, but the explanation given (weight decay, cosine convergence) is a *different* hypothesis being tested. So strictly, this rules out the *combined* H1+recipe configuration, not H1 alone. A reviewer with experimental-design eye will probe.
**Remedy:** "rules out H1 under the original training recipe" or run the same experiment with a longer schedule.

61	\section{Introduction}
62	\label{sec:intro}
63	
64	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions; this paper studies the adapter, holding the backbone fixed.
65	
66	Standard TSFM adapters~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora} pool hidden states and project them to the forecast horizon through the same head for every window (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}a). Real time series mix heterogeneous regimes (quiet baselines, seasonal excursions, abrupt shape changes), and instance normalization handles only the scale-and-offset component of that heterogeneity; residual \emph{shape} heterogeneity (periodicity, trends, level shifts, change points) is encoded in the hidden states across layers, as recent probing analyses of MOMENT and related TSFMs have shown~\citep{wilinski2025tsfm_representations, pandey2025tsfm_semantics}, motivating a per-window mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical: a shared TSFM in GPU memory can serve many tenants through lightweight per-task adapters hot-swapped from host RAM, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
67	
68	This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes (formalized in Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}; signal-ratio diagnostic in Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
69	
70	The failure is specific to instance normalization. Backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), and standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recover at most $10.9\%$ MSE, still $2.7\times$ worse than RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
71	
72	Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix principle is direct. \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen; only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so within a fixed MoE architecture frozen should match or beat unfrozen, which we confirm empirically (frozen best on $4/6$, unfrozen-wins bounded at $\leq 13\%$).
73	
74	\paragraph{Contributions.}
75	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, itemsep=0.15em, topsep=0.2em]
76	\item We identify \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: an architectural failure mode of MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs that is upstream of the optimization-induced collapses targeted by Switch, ST-MoE, ReMoE, and Expert-Choice. Eight causal controls (RevIN ablation, BatchNorm/GroupNorm swaps, router-input interpolation, vision cross-modality, and others) converge on the normalizer as cause.
77	\item We formalize the mechanism via a mutual-information decomposition (Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) and derive a tractable signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ that anticipates which datasets benefit from raw routing at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$), including boundary cases where it correctly does not (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The predictor generalizes across three normalization families, five backbones, and a vision modality.
78	\item We propose RR-MoA, the \textbf{minimal causal intervention}: changing only the router's input from normalized hidden states to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$, with no change to the optimizer, the experts, or the frozen backbone. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against AdaMix (the same architecture with collapsed routing), strictly dominates $11$ optimization-side rescue mechanisms ($720$ runs; best rescue recovers $\leq 10.9\%$, RR-MoA $2.7{\times}$ better), and is not subsumed by naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection ($+42$--$239\%$ MSE; $11/18$ cells re-collapse). Two architecturally distinct variants confirm the principle generalizes beyond RR-MoA's specific router: \textbf{SR-MoA} (self-routed, no external router; outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets) and \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (expert-level raw-input dual-stream; matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets, generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones).
79	\end{itemize}
80

64:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions; this paper studies the adapter, holding the backbone fixed.
232:\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes operate on different parts of the model: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy on the datasets we tested (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).

68:This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the standard MoE adapter recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix} is combined with load balancing~\citep{fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states, and applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes (formalized in Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}; signal-ratio diagnostic in Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
232:\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes operate on different parts of the model: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy on the datasets we tested (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
234:\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Existing diagnoses of routing collapse cluster, broadly, into three threads. \emph{Optimization-side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, and AdaMix's hidden-state router~\citep{wang2022adamix} target training dynamics. \emph{Representation-geometry}: \citet{chi2022representation} attribute collapse to token clustering around expert centroids that emerges \emph{during} routing learning and propose hyperspherical routing scores. \emph{Architectural / paradigm}: \citet{zhou2022expertchoice} invert the token--expert paradigm via Expert-Choice routing, \citet{wang2025remoe} replace top-$k$ with ReLU gating, and \citet{hua2025inputaware} decouple the router from the task objective so that routing decisions reflect input structure rather than task gradients. Our setting differs from all three: the routing signal is structurally absent from the router's input \emph{by design of an upstream architectural normalizer (RevIN)}, before training dynamics, geometry, or routing-paradigm choices can act. Sweeping five rescue families spanning the optimization and routing-paradigm threads (load balancing, z-loss, Expert-Choice, ReLU gating, and entropy regularization) across twelve configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports the broader view that the router's input matters: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
494:\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven new rescue configurations plus the load-balance baseline already in Table~\ref{tab:adamix} (twelve configurations total) on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ run-cells (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
781:\bibitem[Fedus et~al.(2022)]{fedus2022switch}
796:\bibitem[Wang et~al.(2025)]{wang2025remoe}
801:\bibitem[Zhou et~al.(2022)]{zhou2022expertchoice}
846:\bibitem[Zoph et~al.(2022)]{zoph2022stmoe}

228	\section{Related Work}
229	
230	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data: LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; complementary work computes transferability scores for backbone selection (TEMPLATE,~\citealp{zhang2025template}) or provides a unified evaluation suite for general forecasting models (GIFT-Eval,~\citealp{woo2024gifteval}). These works largely vary the finetuning \textit{strategy} (where to inject parameters, what to prune, what to prompt, which transferability score to use) while leaving the per-input adapter \textit{topology} fixed; AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} proposes several adapter variants but selects one per dataset rather than per input window. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window via routing over heterogeneous experts; the closest precedent in spirit is heterogeneous mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting, whereas, to our knowledge, no prior TSFM work uses a per-window mixture over distinct adapter topologies.
231	
232	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes operate on different parts of the model: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy on the datasets we tested (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
233	
234	\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Existing diagnoses of routing collapse cluster, broadly, into three threads. \emph{Optimization-side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, and AdaMix's hidden-state router~\citep{wang2022adamix} target training dynamics. \emph{Representation-geometry}: \citet{chi2022representation} attribute collapse to token clustering around expert centroids that emerges \emph{during} routing learning and propose hyperspherical routing scores. \emph{Architectural / paradigm}: \citet{zhou2022expertchoice} invert the token--expert paradigm via Expert-Choice routing, \citet{wang2025remoe} replace top-$k$ with ReLU gating, and \citet{hua2025inputaware} decouple the router from the task objective so that routing decisions reflect input structure rather than task gradients. Our setting differs from all three: the routing signal is structurally absent from the router's input \emph{by design of an upstream architectural normalizer (RevIN)}, before training dynamics, geometry, or routing-paradigm choices can act. Sweeping five rescue families spanning the optimization and routing-paradigm threads (load balancing, z-loss, Expert-Choice, ReLU gating, and entropy regularization) across twelve configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports the broader view that the router's input matters: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
235	
236	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics. Concurrently, \citet{berthelier2026revin} independently show that several RevIN components are redundant or detrimental, and \citet{zou2025ibnorm} argue that variance-centric normalization (BatchNorm, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) is suboptimal for representation learning because two representations with identical mean and variance can encode different amounts of task-relevant information. We identify a specific, mechanistic consequence of this principle that has not been characterized in the MoE literature: when the stripped statistics carry the \emph{routing} signal in a downstream MoE adapter, instance normalization causes the router to collapse to a single expert under unfreezing, a failure mode distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE rescue literature. Section~\ref{sec:method} formalizes this diagnosis and presents the minimal architectural fix it implies.}
237	
238	\section{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
239	\label{sec:method}

37:\title{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters: A Causal Intervention for Routing Collapse in Time Series Foundation Models}
58:Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with DLinear on $107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
68:This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the standard MoE adapter recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix} is combined with load balancing~\citep{fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states, and applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes (formalized in Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}; signal-ratio diagnostic in Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
72:Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix principle is direct. \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen; only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so within a fixed MoE architecture frozen should match or beat unfrozen, which we confirm empirically (frozen best on $4/6$, unfrozen-wins bounded at $\leq 13\%$).
78:\item We propose RR-MoA, the \textbf{minimal causal intervention}: changing only the router's input from normalized hidden states to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$, with no change to the optimizer, the experts, or the frozen backbone. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against AdaMix (the same architecture with collapsed routing), strictly dominates $11$ optimization-side rescue mechanisms ($720$ runs; best rescue recovers $\leq 10.9\%$, RR-MoA $2.7{\times}$ better), and is not subsumed by naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection ($+42$--$239\%$ MSE; $11/18$ cells re-collapse). Two architecturally distinct variants confirm the principle generalizes beyond RR-MoA's specific router: \textbf{SR-MoA} (self-routed, no external router; outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets) and \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (expert-level raw-input dual-stream; matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets, generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones).
230:\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data: LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; complementary work computes transferability scores for backbone selection (TEMPLATE,~\citealp{zhang2025template}) or provides a unified evaluation suite for general forecasting models (GIFT-Eval,~\citealp{woo2024gifteval}). These works largely vary the finetuning \textit{strategy} (where to inject parameters, what to prune, what to prompt, which transferability score to use) while leaving the per-input adapter \textit{topology} fixed; AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} proposes several adapter variants but selects one per dataset rather than per input window. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window via routing over heterogeneous experts; the closest precedent in spirit is heterogeneous mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting, whereas, to our knowledge, no prior TSFM work uses a per-window mixture over distinct adapter topologies.
234:\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Existing diagnoses of routing collapse cluster, broadly, into three threads. \emph{Optimization-side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, and AdaMix's hidden-state router~\citep{wang2022adamix} target training dynamics. \emph{Representation-geometry}: \citet{chi2022representation} attribute collapse to token clustering around expert centroids that emerges \emph{during} routing learning and propose hyperspherical routing scores. \emph{Architectural / paradigm}: \citet{zhou2022expertchoice} invert the token--expert paradigm via Expert-Choice routing, \citet{wang2025remoe} replace top-$k$ with ReLU gating, and \citet{hua2025inputaware} decouple the router from the task objective so that routing decisions reflect input structure rather than task gradients. Our setting differs from all three: the routing signal is structurally absent from the router's input \emph{by design of an upstream architectural normalizer (RevIN)}, before training dynamics, geometry, or routing-paradigm choices can act. Sweeping five rescue families spanning the optimization and routing-paradigm threads (load balancing, z-loss, Expert-Choice, ReLU gating, and entropy regularization) across twelve configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports the broader view that the router's input matters: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
238:\section{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
363:\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A} (blue): frozen TSFM produces $\mathbf{H}$ for all experts. \textit{Path~B} (orange): Conv1d gate ($853$ params) reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-2 experts; inactive experts (gray) receive no gradient. Standard hidden-state MoE collapses (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
384:\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), spanning pooling families recently shown to span distinct expressivity regimes for transformer-based sequence models across NLP, vision, and time series~\citep{ennadir2025pooling}. Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}). Pseudocode in Algorithm~\ref{alg:rrmoa} (Appendix~\ref{app:setup}). We now empirically validate that this targeted fix recovers routing diversity, beats the seven-method baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear), and that its predictions hold under eight causal controls.
394:\textbf{Baselines.} Seven baselines: three fixed adapters, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), TRACE~\citep{li2025trace}, an independent ensemble of 5 experts, AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} (hidden-state MoE), full fine-tuning (all blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs), and a from-scratch DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} calibration anchor.
401:RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against the seven-baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear).
434:\caption{\textbf{AdaMix routing collapse} (entropy max ${\approx}1.609$). Under unfreezing, entropy ${\to}0.000$. \colorbox{green!8}{Green}: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy ($0.66$--$1.32$), proving RevIN is the causal mechanism. Extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}.}
435:\label{tab:adamix}
462:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why does standard MoE routing fail?} To understand the source of RR-MoA's advantage, we examine AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} adapted to a learned hidden-state softmax router, the standard recipe across modern MoE adapters (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}). Table~\ref{tab:adamix} shows that AdaMix collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy reaches $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations; the remaining 5 stay below $0.55$ (vs.\ max ${\approx}1.61$) with high cross-seed variance ($\sigma$ up to $0.71$), indicating stochastic collapse rather than stable partial routing. The green rows reveal the cause: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$, proving that normalization is the causal mechanism (BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). The causal contrast is sharpest when comparing backbones directly (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL (which lacks RevIN) stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps, opposite trajectories from the same code.}
466:\includegraphics[width=0.92\columnwidth]{figures/adamix_trajectory.pdf}
468:\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}50$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~Dominant expert's gradient grows while others starve (co-adaptation loop, \S\ref{sec:rrmoa}). Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) shows the trajectory inverts.}
494:\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven new rescue configurations plus the load-balance baseline already in Table~\ref{tab:adamix} (twelve configurations total) on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ run-cells (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
496:\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic.} The diagnosis predicts the same fix should work for any router that reads raw input. We validate this with three router-level controls. \textbf{SR-MoA}~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}) drops the external router entirely: each expert has its own sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets ($252$ runs, six backbones) and replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Gating the same SR-MoA architecture on hidden states degrades by $+87$--$768\%$ on all 6 datasets ($p{=}0.016$); on Timer-XL (no RevIN) the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$ ($15$--$100{\times}$ smaller), confirming the effect is normalization-induced. \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}) swaps AdaMix's hidden-state router for a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) extends the principle to the expert level via a shared NLinear raw branch + gated backbone residual, closing the DLinear gap. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls.
508:C1 & RevIN ablation & Collapse is intrinsic to MoE itself, not caused by normalization & Entropy $0.000 \to 0.66$--$1.32$ when RevIN disabled (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} RevIN is \emph{necessary} for collapse \\

388	\subsection{Setup}
389	
390	\textbf{Datasets and tasks.} Six LTSF benchmarks (ETTh1/h2/m1/m2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather, Electricity~\citep{godahewa2021monash}) with chronological splits and channel-wise normalization~\citep{nie2023patchtst}. We evaluate forecasting ($H{=}96$) and imputation ($20\%$ masked).
391	
392	\textbf{Backbones.} Five backbones spanning two normalization families: instance-normalized MOMENT-small/large~\citep{goswami2024moment} (internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}); LayerNorm-only Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE; and Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} as no-instance-normalization negative controls (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). MOMENT-small is primary. Primary results use a strictly frozen backbone; freeze-level ablations follow.
393	
394	\textbf{Baselines.} Seven baselines: three fixed adapters, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), TRACE~\citep{li2025trace}, an independent ensemble of 5 experts, AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} (hidden-state MoE), full fine-tuning (all blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs), and a from-scratch DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} calibration anchor.
395	
396	\textbf{Training and reproducibility.} Adam, MSE loss, 15 epochs; 5 seeds for the core grid, 3 for ablations. Backbone dimensions, full hyperparameters, and per-baseline configurations: Appendix~\ref{app:setup}. All code, raw JSON results, routing-weight logs, and a self-verification script (\texttt{verify.py}) that re-derives every numerical claim from raw data accompany the submission.
397	
398	\subsection{Main Results}
399	\label{sec:main_results}
400	
401	RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against the seven-baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear).
402	
```

462:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why does standard MoE routing fail?} To understand the source of RR-MoA's advantage, we examine AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} adapted to a learned hidden-state softmax router, a recipe widely used in MoE adapters (e.g.,~\citealp{wang2022adamix,fedus2022switch}; Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}). Table~\ref{tab:adamix} shows that AdaMix collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy reaches $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations; the remaining 5 stay below $0.55$ (vs.\ max ${\approx}1.61$) with high cross-seed variance ($\sigma$ up to $0.71$), indicating stochastic collapse rather than stable partial routing. The green rows reveal the cause: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$, proving that normalization is the causal mechanism (BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). The causal contrast is sharpest when comparing backbones directly (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL (which lacks RevIN) stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps, opposite trajectories from the same code.}

1713	\section{Normalization Generalization}
1714	\label{app:norm_generalization}
1715	
1716	To test whether routing collapse is specific to RevIN or a general property of instance-level normalization, we replace MOMENT's RevIN with two alternative normalizers (BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm with 1 group) and run AdaMix with last-4 unfreezing on ETTh1.
1717	
1718	\begin{table}[h]
1719	\centering
1720	\caption{\textbf{Routing collapse generalizes across normalizer types.} All three instance-level normalizers (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm) cause entropy collapse under unfreezing; removing normalization prevents collapse. Per-step trajectories logged over 400 optimizer steps on ETTh1, last-4 unfreezing, seed 42.}
1721	\label{tab:norm_generalization}
1722	\small
1723	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
1724	\toprule
1725	Normalizer & Entropy (step 0) & Entropy (step 400) & Collapses? \\
1726	\midrule
1727	RevIN (default) & $0.507$ & $0.000$ & \textbf{Yes} \\
1728	BatchNorm1d & $0.619$ & $0.004$ & \textbf{Yes} \\
1729	GroupNorm & $0.507$ & $0.000$ & \textbf{Yes} \\
1730	\midrule
1731	None (identity) & $0.623$ & $0.817$ & No \\
1732	\bottomrule
1733	\end{tabular}
1734	\end{table}
1735	
1736	All three normalizers cause entropy collapse to ${\leq}0.004$, while removing normalization entirely keeps entropy stable at $0.817$. The mechanism is the same in each case: the normalizer strips per-window statistics before the encoder, the router receives homogenized hidden states, and gradient co-adaptation amplifies the initial routing bias into full collapse. The mechanism therefore holds across the three instance-level normalizers we tested (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm), not just RevIN. Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts that collapse occurs specifically when the stripped statistics carry routing signal; a cross-modality control on ResNet-18 and ViT-B/16 (Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}) validates this: InstanceNorm1d on ViT patch embeddings \emph{causes} collapse ($0.000\pm0.000$), while InstanceNorm2d on ResNet spatial features does \emph{not}, exactly as the theory predicts.
1737	
1738	\subsection{Cross-Modality Control: Vision MoE}
1739	\label{app:vision_moe}
1740	
1741	To test whether normalization-induced routing collapse transfers beyond time series, we construct a controlled vision experiment: ResNet-18 (pretrained on ImageNet, adapted for CIFAR-10) as backbone, with $K{=}5$ architecturally diverse expert classifiers (mean-pool, max-pool, attention-pool, conv-pool, last-patch, matching the time-series expert pool), no load-balancing loss, and the same four conditions tested in the time-series experiments.

Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, consistent with Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction that low $R$ implies little benefit (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
482	
483	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} The trajectory and causal-contrast figures formalize into a sharp prediction: if normalization-driven gradient co-adaptation is the collapse mechanism, then within a fixed MoE architecture, frozen should match or beat unfrozen. Empirically, frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). The two-factor headline gap (frozen+MoE vs.\ unfrozen+single-head) decomposes into:
484	\emph{(M1) MoE architectural advantage (dominant, $26$--$79\%$):} the best fixed adapter loses to RR-MoA \emph{at the same freeze level} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}), isolating M1 from M2.
485	\emph{(M2) Within-architecture freeze effect (smaller, $\leq 13\%$, non-unidirectional):} within RR-MoA itself, frozen is best on $4/6$ datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two; SR-MoA independently replicates the $4/6$ pattern (Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} attributes M2 to gradient co-adaptation: the Router-Detached Gradient Flow ablation (unfreezes backbone but cuts routing-weighted feedback) degrades MSE by $+35$--$100\%$ on three datasets (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), and disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers full-FT by $54$--$61\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), pinning the mechanism on normalization rather than optimization. A $90$-config hyperparameter tune (LR $10^{-5}$--$10^{-6}$, $100$ epochs, cosine + warmup, layerwise decay, batch up to $512$) still loses by $51$--$71\%$, ruling out optimization confounds.}

483:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} The trajectory and causal-contrast figures formalize into a sharp prediction: if normalization-driven gradient co-adaptation is the collapse mechanism, then within a fixed MoE architecture, frozen should match or beat unfrozen. Empirically, frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). The two-factor headline gap (frozen+MoE vs.\ unfrozen+single-head) decomposes into:
492:{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms it in the full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Independent ensembles are $37$--$46\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}): learned routing, not mere diversity, drives gains.}
526:\label{tab:baselines}
552:\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows): the gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline (k-NN/Ridge diagnostics, Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}), not adapter capacity. Restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes it: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (shared NLinear raw branch $+$ per-expert gated backbone residual; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$) and $107/123$ cells across six backbones; a self-routed SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} (Appendix~\ref{app:sr_ria}) hits the same $6/6$ with $3$ outright wins, and a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) localizes the TSFM's contribution.
1009:\item \textbf{DLinear}~\citep{zeng2023dlinear}: from-scratch supervised; we use the simplified single-Linear variant ($\text{Linear}(L{=}512 \to H{=}96)$, $49$K params), without the trend-seasonal decomposition, as the calibration anchor. Calibration values $0.416 / 0.322 / 0.208$ on ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather (gap to RR-MoA visible in Tables~\ref{tab:rrmoa},~\ref{tab:baselines}).
1245:We sweep LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} across three axes to ensure the comparison in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} is not cherry-picked: rank $r{\in}\{8,16,32\}$, target projections ${\in}\{q{+}v,\; q{+}k{+}v{+}o\}$, and forecast head ${\in}\{$linear, 2-layer MLP$\}$, for 12 configurations $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ 36 runs per dataset (108 runs total across the 3 datasets). All runs use a strictly frozen backbone (matching Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}'s primary setting). The trio \{ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather\} covers an hourly ETT dataset, a 15-min ETT dataset, and a multivariate dataset with $21$ channels; we restrict the LoRA sweep to this trio to keep total compute bounded ($\sim 3.6$ GPU-hours), since the broader 6-dataset grid is already covered for every other baseline in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
1251:\caption{Full LoRA sweep (strictly frozen backbone, 3 seeds, test MSE mean$\pm$std). Bold rows are the best-per-dataset configuration used in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}. No LoRA variant beats the RR-MoA (Top-2) result on any dataset.}

550	RR-MoA outperforms every baseline ($p{<}0.001$). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router) is $36$--$46\%$ worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
551	
552	\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows): the gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline (k-NN/Ridge diagnostics, Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}), not adapter capacity. Restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes it: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (shared NLinear raw branch $+$ per-expert gated backbone residual; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$) and $107/123$ cells across six backbones; a self-routed SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} (Appendix~\ref{app:sr_ria}) hits the same $6/6$ with $3$ outright wins, and a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) localizes the TSFM's contribution.
553	
554	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass and dose-response.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}): the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a phase transition (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; $150$ runs): MSE degrades by $+5.4\%$ for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ but jumps to $+283\%$ at $\alpha{=}1$, with entropy held in $[1.43, 1.57]$ throughout. The threshold is quantitatively predicted at $\alpha^* = R(\mathcal{D})/(R(\mathcal{D})+1) \approx 0.40$ for the median benchmark (Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth}); a temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}
555	
556	
557	\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis.} Each window $\mathbf{x}_i$ decomposes into mean $M_i = \mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$, scale $\Sigma_i = \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i)$, and shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$; RevIN maps $\mathbf{x}_i \mapsto \mathbf{S}_i$ and discards $(M_i, \Sigma_i)$. The mutual-information chain rule quantifies how much routing information lives in the discarded part.

384:\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), spanning pooling families recently shown to span distinct expressivity regimes for transformer-based sequence models across NLP, vision, and time series~\citep{ennadir2025pooling}. Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}). Pseudocode in Algorithm~\ref{alg:rrmoa} (Appendix~\ref{app:setup}). We now empirically validate that this targeted fix recovers routing diversity, beats the seven-method baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear), and that its predictions hold under eight causal controls.
394:\textbf{Baselines.} Seven baselines: three fixed adapters, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), TRACE~\citep{li2025trace}, an independent ensemble of 5 experts, an AdaMix-style hidden-state-routed MoE~\citep{wang2022adamix} (learned softmax router; configuration in Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), full fine-tuning (all blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs), and a from-scratch DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} calibration anchor.
401:RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against the seven-baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear).
535:Ind.\ Ensemble (5 experts) & $1.081 \pm 0.021$ & $1.049 \pm 0.032$ & $0.509 \pm 0.024$ & ${<}0.001^{***}$ \\
550:RR-MoA outperforms every baseline ($p{<}0.001$). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router) is $36$--$46\%$ worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
2037:\textbf{Independent ensemble on Timer-XL.} On MOMENT-small, the independent ensemble (5 experts trained separately, averaged at inference) is $36$--$46\%$ worse than RR-MoA, confirming that learned routing adds value beyond diversity. On Timer-XL the pattern \emph{reverses}: the independent ensemble \emph{wins} $4/6$ datasets over both AdaMix and RR-MoA (ETTh1 $0.462$, ETTm1 $0.384$, ETTm2 $0.296$ vs AdaMix $0.477$, $0.399$, $0.326$). When hidden-state routing is healthy (no RevIN), simple averaging is a strong baseline that learned routing does not consistently beat. This further confirms RR-MoA's advantage is specifically about rescuing collapsed routing under RevIN.

494	\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven new rescue configurations plus the load-balance baseline already in Table~\ref{tab:adamix} (twelve configurations total) on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ run-cells (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.

1046:\caption{\textbf{Inference latency and memory across all three architectural variants} (MOMENT-small, A10G GPU, batch size $128$, $200$ repeats with $20$-step CUDA-event warmup; synthetic random inputs at the standard LTSF batch shape, since latency for non-autoregressive deterministic forward passes is data-oblivious). All three architectural fixes (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) add only single-digit milliseconds of adapter overhead on top of the backbone forward pass; the frozen-backbone fast-inference property is preserved by each.}
1472:\caption{\textbf{SR-MoA architecture (Self-Routed Mixture of Adapters).} Each of $K{=}5$ experts has its own sigmoid gate $\sigma_k$ reading $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ directly, with no external router. The frozen TSFM produces $\mathbf{H}$, and each expert's contribution is scaled by its self-gate. SR-MoA shares only the \emph{raw-input} principle with RR-MoA (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}): the routing architecture is otherwise entirely different. SR-MoA outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets, providing architecture-agnostic validation that the principle, not the specific router, drives the gains.}
1478:\caption{\textbf{Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA): routing input control} (dense, 5 seeds unless noted). Each expert has its own sigmoid gate, with no external router. \textbf{Top}: gating on raw input vs hidden states (frozen, MOMENT-small). \textbf{Second}: Timer-XL negative control with no RevIN, so raw$\approx$hidden. \textbf{Third}: freeze-level ablation confirms Frozen Paradox (frozen best 4/6). \textbf{Bottom}: cross-backbone generalization (H=96, frozen). 252 runs total.}
1479:\label{tab:self_routed}
